Examples
C5
Screening aid without autonomy of the digital solution2
Offers a disease or impairment screening aid via the production of options proposed to the patient or healthcare professional. The digital solutions concerned are intended to be used in a large population and involve diagnostic confirmation, where applicable.
This category does not include systems providing general non-personalised health advice.
Diabetic retinopathy screening software
Connected genetic disease or cancer detection system
C6
Diagnostic aid without autonomy of the digital solution
Offers a personalised disease or health state diagnostic aid via the production of diagnostic options or prognostic information intended for the healthcare professional.
This category does not include systems that only provide a non-personalised list of symptoms and signs of a disease.
System using patient data to assist a healthcare professional in diagnosing a disease (e.g. cancer) or health state (e.g. pregnancy confirmation and dating). Systems providing prognostic information fall under this category. For example:
− Software for detecting tumours using imaging techniques
− Software associated with a chest band to detect breathing pauses with a view to diagnosing sleep apnoea
− Medical pathology result-based diagnostic aid software
2 A non-autonomous solution refers to a technology operating in an open circuit, i.e. a solution that will not carry out any therapeutic, screening, or diagnostic action without prior human interven-tion.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 14
C7
Treatment aid without autonomy of the digital solution
Enables treatment, determination of parameters for implementation or guidance of the medical decision. The technology is used during care provision or beforehand to optimise implementation.
This category does not include systems intended for therapeutic decision-making.
System that uses the patient's clinical data to calculate treatment parameters (for example in radiotherapy) or treatment administration methods (infusion rate, dilutions, etc.) directly. Depending on the circumstances, the users may be those implementing the treatment, i.e. patients or prescribers, those dispensing it (pharmacists, assistants) or those administering it (nurse). For example:
− Application calculating insulin dose based on blood glucose data
− Dosage adaptation software based on kidney function
System used by healthcare professionals for the purposes of aiding treatment, regardless of type (surgical, rehabilitation, etc.). For example:
− Gamification solution used during care provision in the presence of a healthcare professional (for example, application in the context of locomotor or oculomotor rehabilitation)
− Pelvic floor rehabilitation solution used in the context of sessions conducted in the presence of a healthcare professional
− Orthopaedic surgery guidance solution, etc.
C8
Therapeutic decision-making aid
Suggests, based on the patient's data, one or more options to the professional to help them make therapeutic decisions relative to a diagnosed condition.
This category does not include systems that provide non-personalised general health advice or systems intended to improve the care pathway.
System that uses the patient's data in order to propose the various possible treatment options to healthcare professionals (for example, in oncology). The users are those who prescribe and dispense the treatment.
D1
Autonomous decision management
Analyses data, establishes the diagnosis then automatically and autonomously adjusts the treatment to be administered or implemented.
This category does not include systems incorporating the functions of this category but operating in an open circuit.
System that uses the patient's data with a view to diagnosing a disease status and subsequently automatically modifying, without prior human intervention, the parameters of a treatment.
At the present time, the examples of this type of fully autonomous technologies appear to be limited.
For example:
− Implantable cardiac defibrillator with a remote monitoring solution that analyses data from a heart monitor, delivers a shock in the event of cardiac arrest, and can transmit alerts to the healthcare professional responsible for the patient's care.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 15
2. Analysis of the literature
2.1. Methodology
The literature search was limited to publications in English and French over the period from April 2017 to May 2022. The search concerned the following: websites, technology assessments, recommendations and guidelines, consensus conferences, guides, meta-analyses, systematic reviews and clinical studies. Literature monitoring was then conducted until September 2022.
The search was conducted by consulting:
‒ several bibliographic databases, including Medline and various websites, including the Cochrane Library;
‒ websites publishing guidelines;
‒ websites publishing technology assessment reports;
‒ the websites of learned societies.
2.2. Objectives
The objectives of the systematic review were to identify:
‒ the different types of DMD under development for each of the main specialities concerned and the main therapeutic areas concerned;
‒ the first evaluations of the metrological performances of these DMDs;
‒ the positions of regulators and health technology assessment agencies.
2.3. Search results
2.3.1. Volumes
A total of 945 bibliographic references were identified from the systematic search performed on Medline and 37 on websites. Of these references, 318 were analysed and 49 were retained. The selection process is illustrated in figure 1 below.
Figure 1. Article selection flow chart

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 16
2.3.2. Key takeaways from the literature
2.3.2.1. Type of digital medical devices for professional use identified
Due to the heterogeneity, the speed of evolution and/or innovation and the sometimes ephemeral nature of these technologies, it is not possible to produce an exhaustive map of them. An overview of the main development trends for this type of tool was produced via a systematic review of the scientific literature.
Considering the different product categories, the main DMDs for professional use highlighted by the literature review are diagnostic or screening aid systems and medical decision support systems (3). These systems can correspond to a digital form of validated decision trees used in clinical practice, but they can also be based on more complex models, such as deep learning (4).
As far as clinical uses or applications are concerned, at the present time, diagnostic or screening support systems using deep learning focus primarily on imaging (X-rays, scans, photos, etc.) and concern disciplines such as radiology, oncology, pulmonology, dermatology, anatomical pathology, gastroenterology or ophthalmology. In particular, these applications include diagnostic or screening aids for:
‒ pulmonary nodules or tuberculosis based on chest X-rays (5, 6);
‒ cancerous nodules based on mammograms (7);
‒ melanomas based on skin lesion images (8, 9);
‒ bone fractures based on X-rays (10);
‒ diabetic retinopathy based on eye fundus exams (11);
‒ polyps during colonoscopy (12).
It should be noted that there are also intermediate image processing software solutions, with or without AI, used at an intermediate stage between recording of measurements and diagnosis: this is the case for software with AI to improve the resolution of scan images and reduce background noise in radiology, for example (13).
Medical decision support systems can be used to calculate the parameters or methods of implementing a treatment directly from the patient's data. Other usages for the prediction of therapeutic response are also developing.
More rarely, DMDs based on virtual and augmented reality technologies are found in surgery, with the objective of supporting or simulating surgical procedures. That is the case for surgical navigation software used in orthopaedic surgery, for example (14).
No digital medical devices for professional use integrating autonomous medical decision management were identified in this exploratory analysis.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 17
2.3.2.2. Barriers to the use of these digital medical devices in routine clinical practice
Despite a growing number of publications on DMDs for professional use, their use and adoption by professionals is still limited in routine clinical practice  (3).
In addition to issues related to the performance of DMDs, there are several barriers to their adoption by professionals:
‒ the positioning and integration of DMDs in the care pathway and in the workflow of healthcare professionals (15). In reality, these tools may involve changes in patient care or in the practices of healthcare professionals;
‒ the time required to use these tools, in addition to difficulties learning how to use them if their use is not sufficiently intuitive and ergonomic(3) ;
‒ the lack of clinical relevance of the information provided in view of the amount of medical information that is not useful in practice (4). Hence, some information from DMDs cannot be directly transposed into useful medical actions (16) and the risk of multiplying unnecessary or inappropriate alerts can lead to a desensitisation to warnings (4);
‒ the limited assistance provided by such tools in the absence of clinical consensus. In reality, for medical decision support DMDs, in the absence of a consensus for the management of a given case, the clinical value of the DMD will be limited, not to mention the difficulties related to training and evaluation of the tool (3);
‒ conversely, the lack of explainability of the result produced by the DMD, leading to a  “black box” effect (3). Indeed, a result based on a complex mathematical prediction can be a major barrier in terms of healthcare professionals' confidence in DMDs (15);
‒ the fear of malfunction of the device (3). The slightest error due to the use of a DMD for professional use will result in significant negative perceptions (16).
2.3.2.3. Technologies with heterogeneous performances
Once the digital medical device for professional use has been developed, its performance can be determined:
• in theoretical terms with a performance based on a test sample;
• in real-world conditions, where the DMD must be integrated into the clinical environment and be the subject of on-site adjustments (sensitivity/specificity).
These theoretical and real-world performances are liable to vary due to different factors, leading to a lack of external validity of the algorithms:
‒ the volume of training data required to train artificial intelligence algorithms is sometimes insufficient, leading software developers to reuse historical data of variable quality or labelled in a heterogeneous manner (17);
‒ the representative bias of the sample used for artificial intelligence algorithm training. The sample may be representative of one type of population, but the performance of use of the DMD will not be widely extrapolable to different populations. This is the case, for example, with certain DMDs used to help diagnose skin cancer in dermatology: if the algorithm is trained mainly on photos of “white populations”, it will be less effective on coloured skins (18);

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 18
‒ the risk of training the model based on confounding factors, overfitting3 and the failure to take into account the patient's environmental context during training (16, 19). Furthermore, as the training samples for these algorithms contain little or no clinical data, the algorithms may not be very appropriate or extrapolable to routine clinical practice;
‒ to finish, the quality of the input data is crucial and may imply the need to standardise the performance of procedures and interventions to ensure the good performance of the DMD in real-world conditions. For example, in the detection of melanoma in dermatology using smartphone photographs, variations in angle, light or image quality can significantly alter the performance of the DMD (20);
‒ in addition, vigilance must also be exercised with regard to  potential programming errors or inadequate system maintenance, which could lead to a DMD that quickly becomes obsolete in practice (4).
A certain amount of information required to answer questions on these important dimensions in the performance of a digital MD integrating one or more learning functions is taken into account in the HAS grid produced in 2020 “
Grille descriptive des fonctionnalités des dispositifs médicaux embarquant un système avec apprentissage automatique (intelligence artificielle)
” (21) [Descriptive grid of functions of medical devices with an embedded machine learning system (artificial intelligence)].
2.3.2.4. The need for robust studies
Meta-analyses and systematic reviews have highlighted the lack of robust studies to assess the performance of DMDs for professional use (22-24). This is primarily due to the methodological limitations of the studies carried out, justified by software developers by the specificities of digital technology (25).
Firstly, there are few comparative studies of machines versus healthcare professionals or between different algorithms (19, 25). Where they do exist, for example for diagnostic aid systems, it is noted that the approach to assessing the diagnostic precision of artificial intelligence models does not reflect clinical practice (22). Indeed, these are evaluated in isolation without taking into account the clinical setting in which the tools are used. In addition, some publications have revealed weaknesses in the methodology of these studies, such as the lack of sample calculation or the use of different test datasets between the groups compared (17, 22, 25).
Currently, studies to assess the performance of DMDs are mainly retrospective, based on a pre-assembled dataset from a single site (17, 25, 26). The advantage is that they contain routine clinical data. However, the quality of these data is often questionable: clinical data are routinely collected from patient records and medical reports (e.g. radiology or histology reports). These datasets, not initially intended for use in these studies, are therefore not harmonised (operator-dependent) and contain many missing data and potential biases (25).
Finally, regardless of the type of study, most of these models are only evaluated internally, i.e. the test data sample is drawn from the same sample as the training data. “Since the lack of validation of the algorithm on an independent dataset does not presume the extrapolation of results to other populations, this may lead to an overestimation of the performance of artificial intelligence models relative to real conditions of use” (25).
3 Overfitting corresponds to a common problem in machine learning where the model has high accuracy when tested on data from the same source as its training data, but its performance is not extended to novel sources of data (19).

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 19
Lastly, algorithm evaluation studies are primarily focused on performance. However, there are different levels of evaluation. In addition to the theoretical evaluation of performance, an evaluation of performance in real-world situations may be necessary, along with an evaluation of the clinical and/or organisational value provided by the solution in routine clinical practice. The clinical and/or organisational impact of an algorithm may be limited despite an excellent performance. For example, a diagnostic aid system with high sensitivity and lower specificity results in more false positives and therefore a potential wasting of time for healthcare professionals. Its relevance will depend on the use context. The question of the real benefit of these tools for patients is also raised. The choice of the performance indicator is therefore crucial in the construction of an algorithm (27).
This is the case, for example, with tools to help diagnose skin cancers, which are configured with a high sensitivity in order to detect a greater number of melanomas. An algorithm may then wrongly categorise a larger number of benign nevi as malignant (false positives). Diagnoses based on tools of this type could therefore lead to unnecessary procedures and biopsies, causing harm to patients (19, 28).
Very recently, an expert consensus proposed guidelines (named DECIDE-AI) for the early-stage clinical evaluation of decision support systems driven by artificial intelligence (29) and included other published guidelines for the evaluation of artificial intelligence algorithms (TRIPOD-AI, STARD-AI, SPIRIT-AI and CONSORT-AI) recommended depending on the algorithm development stages (2). Hence, these guidelines are intended to improve the standardisation of development plans for the evaluation of decision support systems.
Figure 2. Comparison of development pathways for drug therapies, AI in healthcare and surgical innovation, Vasey et al., 2022 (29)
Existing guidelines relative to study designs for the evaluation of artificial intelligence algorithms are summarised in Figure 3 below.
− The TRIPOD-AI and STARD-AI guidelines concern the evaluation of performances and can be used at the preclinical stage.
− DECIDE-AI is the guideline developed by Vasey et al. (29) to evaluate the clinical utility and safety of the algorithm while taking into account real conditions of use (human factors) from the early stage of development of an artificial intelligence algorithm.
− Finally, the SPIRIT-AI and CONSORT-IA guidelines concern phase 3 of clinical development and advocate the implementation of comparative and prospective study designs to evaluate artificial intelligence algorithms as gold standards.
− It should be noted that no consensus has yet been proposed for the evaluation of artificial intelligence algorithms in the post-marketing surveillance phase.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 20
Figure 3. Guidelines for the evaluation of artificial intelligence algorithms, Vasey et al., 2022 (29)
2.3.2.5. The position of regulators, health technology assessment agencies and other academic players
2.3.2.5.1. Existing classifications of digital solutions according to their risk level
Given the diversity of technologies and functions of DMDs for professional use, their classification using a risk matrix would appear to be useful in order to assess the risks associated with their use.
The literature did not reveal any classification of DMDs for professional use as a function of their risk level. However, three existing digital solution risk frameworks, not specific to DMDs for professional use, were identified.
‒ Classification of Software as a Medical Device (FDA)
The first risk classification concerns software with medical device status or Software as a Medical Device (SaMD) (30). It results from the work of the International Medical Device Regulators Forum (IMDRF), a working group of medical device regulators from around the world. A SaMD is defined as software intended to be used for one or more medical purposes that perform these purposes without being part of a hardware medical device. Risk categorisation primarily aims to allocate a risk score to a SaMD in order to determine the level of information to be provided by the manufacturer in terms of quality control or clinical evaluation. The risk levels, presented in Table 2, are ranked from I to IV (the fourth being the most critical risk) and are based on the state of the healthcare situation or condition (critical, serious, non-serious) and significance of the information provided by the SaMD to the healthcare decision (treat or diagnose, drive clinical management and inform clinical management). This classification was adopted by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) in its December 2017 guideline entitled (30) “
Software as a Medical Device (SaMD):
 Clinical Evaluation
” aimed at defining recommendations concerning the clinical evaluation of SaMDs to demonstrate their safety, efficacy and performance.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 21
Table 2. Risk categorisation for SaMDs, FDA, 2018 (30)
This framework provides some indications on the highest-risk intended uses: in decreasing order of risks, MDs for diagnostic or therapeutic use, then clinical decision support, then clinical decision information. However, it is not specific to software aimed at healthcare professionals and does not differentiate between users of the MD, patients or healthcare professionals. In addition, categorisation of the condition or state of health as a function of three seriousness levels appears to be highly subjective. In fact, the criteria to be taken into account to assess the criticality are not defined. Finally, the exclusion of software embedded with a hardware MD also raises questions with a view to using this framework for DMDs for professional use.
In parallel, the FDA has adapted its regulatory oversight to the specificities of SaMDs, particularly for those incorporating artificial intelligence. Hence, in the context of its Pre-Cert (31) program, the FDA proposes a new regulatory paradigm for the market access of software with artificial intelligence developed by companies pre-certified on the basis of quality criteria such as organisational excellence and who are committed to monitoring the real-world performance of their software solution(s). This continuous monitoring aims to ensure that safety and efficacy are maintained throughout the life cycle of the product and paves the way for algorithmic adaptations after the product has been placed on the market (32), as long as these planned changes were specified in the pre-marketing application.
‒ Evidence Standards Framework for Digital Health Technologies (NICE)
In its document entitled “
Evidence Standards Framework for Digital Health Technologies
” (33), updated in August 2022, NICE proposes a classification of digital solutions stratified based on the potential risks to health system users and to the system and to associate this with a required level of evidence for each functional level of the classification.
This classification includes three broad risk levels, called “Tiers”:
‒ Tier A: digital solutions used for administrative or management functions that do not directly affect patients but provide benefits to the health system;
‒ Tier B: digital solutions intended to help patients manage their disease:
• providing information to users on health conditions or health promotion advice,
• enabling patients to track their health (diary, etc.),
• communication between healthcare professionals;
‒ Tier C: digital solutions intended for patients and professionals used as clinical decision support systems or for treatment or diagnosis. This tier is divided into four categories depending on the functions of the solutions:

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 22
• transmission of clinical information,
• clinical decision support,
• diagnosis support,
• treatment support.
Tier C uses the classification proposed by the IMDRF and is based on both the nature of the information provided by the digital solution and the state of healthcare situation or condition.
Therefore, this risk framework does not only concern DMDs for professional use, which are, in fact, all grouped in the "Tier C" category, the highest risk level, and does not allow the use of this risk framework as it stands to differentiate DMDs for professional use as a function of the risks associated with their use.
Figure 4. Classification of digital solutions according to NICE, 2022 (33)
‒ Functional classification of digital solutions according to their intended use (HAS)
Finally, in February 202, the HAS published (2) the “
Functional classification, according to their intended use, of digital solutions used in the context of medical and paramedical care
”. This classification includes 11 types of digital solutions grouped into four levels (A, B, C, D), depending on their intended use, supplemented by the capacity of the digital solution to take into account user/patient parameters and the autonomous or non-autonomous nature of the digital solution, making it possible to differentiate between technologies requiring human intervention to carry out a therapeutic, screening, or diagnostic action, and those generating this type of action on their own, i.e. without prior human intervention. This classification was designed primarily as a core reference tool for the various potential players and for multiple usages. It could be used as part of a matrix approach.
In the various classifications already produced, there has been an evolution in the level of detail between the FDA, NICE and HAS classifications. The FDA classification is based on very “macro”-type criteria, whereas NICE refines its classification by increasing the number of differentiated functions but

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 23
does not separate digital solutions according to whether or not they include embedded artificial intelligence. Finally, the HAS classification has the greatest precision in terms of functions and differentiates according to the type of user (healthcare professionals, patients and caregivers). It also separates solutions with or without embedded artificial intelligence into different categories. The HAS indicated from the outset that this classification was intended to be further developed over time on the basis of its uses.
DMDs for professional use are not the subject of a specific category within the different classifications outlined above. However, the classification proposed by the HAS, constructed with a view to being reused to integrate a matrix component, could be serve as a basis for the development of a potential risk matrix, or even an assessment matrix, within the framework of this project, depending on future exchanges.
It should be noted that in November 2016 (34), the HAS published “
Good practice guidelines on health apps and smart devices (mobile health or mhealth
”. Although the scope of these guidelines concerns apps or smart devices that have potential effects on health but are not medical devices, the HAS considered that apps and smart devices used by healthcare professionals, even if they do not have a medical purpose announced by the operator, already had the highest "criticality level". In view of the use of DMDs for professional use as clinical decision support tools, this once again raises the question of the potential risk of using these tools without genuinely established evidence of their safety and performance in real-world conditions.
2.3.2.5.2. Publications on methods to help users make choices
Recently, the NHS AI Lab published a buyer’s guide for these technologies aimed at healthcare organisations and professionals (35), coupled with an assessment template (36, 37). The objective of this assessment template is to set out the important questions that need to be considered in order to make well-informed buying decisions about AI devices. As for the guide itself, it provides decision support for the selection of a DMD by enabling the buyer to consider the regulatory aspects of the product, the validation of the model and its integration into clinical practice.
The questionnaire contains almost one hundred questions, which are found in the associated evaluation template, including questions such as:
− What problem are you trying to solve, and is artificial intelligence the right solution?
− Does this product meet regulatory standards?
− Does this product perform in line with the manufacturer's claims?
− Will this product work in practice?
− Can you secure the support you need from staff and service users?
− Can you build and maintain a culture of ethical responsibility around this project?
− What data protection protocols do you need to safeguard privacy and comply with the law?
− Can you manage and maintain this product after you adopt it?
− Is your procurement process fair, transparent and competitive?
− Can you ensure a commercially and legally robust contractual outcome for your organisation, and the health and care sector?
For the technical aspects of the software and questions relative to the development and validation of the tool, this guide can be compared to the “
Grille descriptive des fonctionnalités des dispositifs médicaux embarquant un système avec apprentissage automatique (intelligence artificielle)
”

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 24
[Descriptive grid of functions of medical devices with an embedded machine learning system (artificial intelligence)], produced in 2020 (21). The purpose of this grid is to list the information required from companies in the context of a request for reimbursement under common law for medical devices with embedded artificial intelligence and to provide a structural framework for any manufacturer to describe, in particular, the role of each function concerned, the characteristics of the data used at the various stages of the product's development, the results obtained and the way the algorithm works. It addresses the key points, grouped by categories, which cover the entire process of developing a technology: the claimed use, the training data used, the type of learning of the algorithm, its re-learning frequency, the place given to human intervention in this process or the performance thresholds. This grid is above all descriptive, built to overcome the “black box” effect of artificial intelligence algorithms. It is not a substitute for assessment of utility.
The comparison between the NHS and HAS grids, presented in Table 4, highlights that beyond the technical description of the artificial intelligence algorithm, the NHS grid focuses more on the whole life cycle of the MD, such as the regulatory and contractual aspects or the integration into practice and the associated organisational impact.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 25

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 26
In the same vein, in 2020, an expert group set up by the European Commission (the High-Level Expert Group on Artificial Intelligence (AI HLEG)) published a checklist designed to assess the reliability of artificial intelligence algorithms in health, named ALTAI, for the purposes of self-assessment. A variety of categories are covered in the checklist, such as data reliability, data confidentiality and the explainability of the algorithm, i.e. that the decisions taken by the algorithm can be explained to users. The checklist is the result of work carried out by Australian researchers aimed at helping healthcare professionals to assess whether an artificial intelligence algorithm can be used in routine care and to identify the technical difficulties to be overcome. The checklist led to the development of consensus guidelines in 2022, called “FUTURE-AI”, by an international network of researchers and inter-disciplinary experts. These guidelines are organised into six categories: fairness, universality, traceability, usability, robustness and explainability, and are aimed at developers, evaluators and regulators (28).
In September 2022, the FDA also published its “Clinical Decision Support Software: guidance for industry and Food and Drug administration Staff” (38). This guidance is complementary to previous publications (39) and helps define the scope of the FDA’s oversight of clinical decision support software, particularly that intended for healthcare professionals, that does not have medical device status. However, it is not intended to establish regulatory requirements for this type of tool. Nonetheless, this guidance presents recommendations relative to the information to be supplied to healthcare professionals by software developers. The aim is to ensure that healthcare professionals make medical decisions based on the recommendations presented the software, as well as on the basis of their own judgement and knowledge. An algorithm must therefore be explainable and the healthcare professional must be able to question the recommendations of this algorithm thanks to certain information that must be provided to the professional. The information to be provided includes information on the quality and relevance of the patient data used by the software or the degree of personalisation of the information provided by the software and how it is specific to a patient and taken into account in the proposed decision support.
Finally, in France, the National Agency for the Assessment of Health and Medico-Social System Performance (ANAP) developed a platform of artificial intelligence solutions in the field of healthcare in May 2022, to reference examples of digital solutions with AI aimed at healthcare facilities or professionals. This descriptive directory collates examples of solutions with AI intended to facilitate the organisation and management of care, patient follow-up or solutions to assist diagnosis or decision-making. No evaluation of the referenced devices is carried out by ANAP in this context, the objective being to put in place a descriptive showcase of solutions with AI that can be used in hospitals.
In parallel, the opening of the
“Bouquet de services”
4 digital platform aimed at healthcare professionals at the end of 2022 can provide decision support insofar as it is intended to simplify access to various digital services on condition that they meet interoperability, safety and ethical criteria. With the same objective, in the USA, a directory of medical decision support or diagnostic/screening support systems meeting interoperability and standardisation criteria has been set up by the Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality (AHRQ) (40).
No specific publications on the use of virtual or augmented reality in surgery were identified.
4 G-NIUS. Bouquet de services: the “Bouquet de services” or “service package” allows healthcare professionals to access several services at a single point. [Online] 2022.
https://gnius.esante.gouv.fr/fr/reglementation/fiches-reglementation/bouquet-de-services

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 27
2.3.2.6. Evaluations performed
The review of the literature reveals little evaluation work conducted by regulators or assessment agencies on DMDs for professional use.
The first technological assessments of these devices were performed in the United Kingdom and Canada and are presented in Table 3.
‒ In particular, NICE produced several assessments between 2020 and 2022 (four published) concerning diagnosis support or medical decision support tools. The NICE assessments, whose precise purpose is not explicitly defined (in practice, information, funding or proper use), are based on a (non-exhaustive) inventory of available technologies and a critical analysis of available clinical studies and examine the different costs and economic models proposed, along with expert opinions.
‒ The CADTH conducted an evaluation of clinical decision support systems in medical imaging in 2019 (41). In its report, it indicates that the available clinical evidence is limited due, in particular, to the methodological weaknesses of the studies analysed.
‒ In its 2021 report on remote diabetic retinopathy screening, the INESSS conducted a critical analysis of the various systems available. This report describes in detail for each device the technology used, the associated clinical data, and the costs involved in its use. There is a particular focus on the evaluation of the efficiency of these devices and the impact of their integration on the organisation of care.
Report on remote diabetic retinopathy screening using an artificial intelligence solution, INESSS
“The integration of AI within a remote diabetic retinopathy screening programme has major impacts in technological (e.g. interoperability, percentage of unanalysable images), clinical (e.g. sensitivity and specificity), organisational (e.g. timely access to care), economic (efficiency), ethical (e.g. fairness, confidentiality and data security) and human (e.g. patient experience, clinician experience) terms. Data from the literature show that most studies only evaluate the technical and clinical performance of AI solutions for diabetic retinopathy screening, and usually in an experimental setting. The challenges of using AI solutions for diabetic retinopathy screening in a real-world care and service setting therefore remain under-explored.”

2.3.2.7. Assessment perspectives and oversight proposals
Several publications proposing perspectives for the assessment or oversight of DMDs for professional use are beginning to emerge.
Hence the European Parliament published a report entitled “Artificial intelligence in healthcare: application, risks and ethical and societal impacts” in June 2022. Seven policy options are outlined to better develop, evaluate, deploy and exploit technically, clinically and ethically sound AI algorithms in health systems. These include:
− promoting the co-creation of AI algorithms by developers, healthcare professionals and/or patients;
− creating an “AI passport” associated with algorithms aimed at enhancing transparency and trust, presenting information relative to the model, the data used, the evaluation (biases, limitations, extreme cases, etc.), usage (e.g. statistical distributions, (dis)agreements with clinicians, identified failures, memory usage, etc.) and maintenance (last updates, software versioning, last periodic evaluation, dates, etc.);
− developing a legal framework concerning medical accountability;
− improving training of healthcare professionals in digital technology and information provided to the general public with the aim of encouraging the adoption of these tools and minimising errors in their use;
− implementing a strategy aimed at reducing the European divide in medical AI.
This work can be compared to the “Artificial intelligence in healthcare: Benefits and Challenges of Machine Learning Technologies for Medical Diagnostics” report published in September 2022 (49) by

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 29
the American National Academy of Medicine. Three major strategic perspectives for policymakers are detailed to improve the development of tools and limit the challenges associated with diagnostic technologies incorporating AI:
− the production of guidance, standards or best practices for evaluation of these types of technologies by policymakers;
− facilitating access to high-quality medical datasets with the aim of developing and testing technologies incorporating AI. The objective of high-quality databases is to facilitate training and testing of AI algorithms across diverse conditions, representative of real-world situations;
− finally, the National Academy of Medicine promotes collaboration among developers, providers, and regulators for the integration of these technologies into medical practice and their evaluation through the co-creation of these tools.
In summary
The main digital medical devices for professional use found in the literature correspond to systems designed to aid diagnosis or screening and medical decision support systems.
Analysis of the increasingly prolific literature on DMDs for professional use reveals:
‒ a use of DMDs using AI that is still sporadic in routine clinical practice;
‒ a heterogeneity in terms of the performance of the medical devices proposed, in particular due to a potential lack of reliability or representativeness of the training data used;
‒ a need for genuinely prospective real-world studies, to validate and compare DMDs;
‒ the absence of a ready-to-use risk matrix to prioritise assessment needs for DMDs for professional use;
‒ the first evaluation frameworks for these devices.
The studies on DMDs for professional use carried out by regulators or health technology assessment agencies are still limited but are developing. To date, only the INESSS and NICE have evaluated some of these devices, based on a critical analysis of the available clinical studies and different costs and economic models proposed. In particular, the INESSS analysed the efficiency of these devices and the impact of their integration on the organisation of care.
The NHS has published a decision guide for users or buyers of DMDs for professional use with AI. In addition to a description of the data, the model and the characteristics, this guide enables users to consider the main challenges associated with the use of these tools, and, in particular, the integration in the pathway and the associated organisational impact, ethical issues, acceptance by teams, comparison with other existing solutions, as well as questions that are essential for buyers, such as issues related to maintenance and after-sales service, interoperability, etc.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 30
3. Survey via INAHTA members
3.1. Methodology
In order to identify the position of assessment agencies on the subject of DMDs for professional use, a survey within the INAHTA – International Network of Agencies for Health Technology Assessment – (49 assessment agencies – 30 countries) was conducted.
The questionnaire was shared with the network’s agencies and aimed to identify:
‒ the current position of agencies and, in particular, whether they evaluate this type of medical device and, if they do, the applicable criteria and assessment conditions (optional or mandatory);
‒ ongoing or scheduled work on the theme.
The analysis was limited to the data provided by agencies having completed the questionnaire.
3.2. Results
A total of ten agencies from seven countries completed the questionnaire shared on the INAHTA: G-BA and IQWiG in Germany, AIHTA and GoeG in Austria, CADTH in Canada, AQuAS in Spain, FinCCHTA in Finland, ZonMW and ZIN in the Netherlands and AOTMiT in Poland.
Among the organisations that responded to the questionnaire, the diversity of responses reflects the heterogeneity of assessment agencies' positions on digital health in general.
An analysis of the contributions shows that all respondents are likely to be involved in the evaluation of DMDs for professional use:
‒ at the request of the Ministry of Health (in the Netherlands, in Poland);
‒ on an optional basis (DMDs for professional use associated with a hospital procedure in Germany; in the process of being set up in Austria; in Canada; in Finland);
‒ on a mandatory basis, especially if funding cover is sought (in Germany for DMDs for professional use associated with a non-hospital procedure; in Spain in theory).
However, to date, few assessments of DMDs for professional use have been reported by respondents. Respondents also indicate that they have not set up a specific assessment framework for DMDs for professional use, without a user-specific approach to digital technology. When evaluations are carried out, they are based on methodological frameworks appropriate to any medical device, digital or otherwise.
For example, in Finland, the Digi-HTA evaluation method can be used for all types of digital MD. The objective of this evaluation is to issue a recommendation for use (with a score from 0 to 10) of a digital solution, which is made public. A score ≤ 4 corresponds to a recommendation to give careful consideration before using the product (an organisation considering the deployment of a product should consider that there are shortcomings in one or more of the key areas) whereas a score of 10 corresponds to a recommendation to use the product.
Where they are performed, these evaluations are primarily based on clinical criteria. However, several countries have also indicated basing their evaluation on ethical, legal and social criteria (Germany, Spain, Poland), costs (Finland, Spain, Poland) or organisational impact (Spain). Two countries assess data security and protection; via a certificate (Poland) or via an expert assessment (Finland).

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 31
Specific working groups examining these themes have been set up in a number of countries, with consideration of development of specific frameworks (Austria, Spain) or the provision of methodological guides for users (Netherlands).
All the positions of the agencies are summarised in the annexes.
In summary
Although all INAHTA respondents are liable to evaluate DMDs for professional use, in practice, there have been very few assessments by network members to date for these types of technologies. No INHATA partners have produced a specific framework for the assessment of DMDs for professional use. Some agencies consider that the conventional assessment methods apply. Some agencies are considering developing a specific framework or publishing methodological guides aimed at the users of DMDs for professional use.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 32
4. Stakeholder interviews
4.1. Methodology
Today, all medical professions are concerned by digital technology.
In order to assess the digital needs of healthcare professionals and their potential need for assistance in the selection of digital medical devices for professional use, bilateral meetings were held with the national professional councils most directly concerned by the subject, as well as with experts or institutions. When selecting the specialities contacted, particular care was taken to include clinical and technical specialities.
The aim of these informal interviews was to cover the following themes:
• What types of digital medical devices are used by professionals?
• What are the intended uses of these devices: screening support, diagnostic support, treatment support, etc.?
• What technology is used and what is the role of the healthcare professional?
• How are decisions made and DMDs selected by healthcare professionals?
Due to the informal nature of the interviews, the results are presented in summary form.
4.2. Results
4.2.1. Quantitative results
The national professional councils (CNPs) consulted were particularly responsive and are actively engaged in digital issues.
A total of 28 interviews were held between April 2022 and October 2022, broken down as follows:
‒ 18 national professional councils;
‒ 7 experts or stakeholders: 3 hospital pharmacists, 2 radiologists, 1 rhythmologist, 1 young healthcare professionals group;
‒ 2 institutional structures;
‒ 1 user and patient representative association.
4.2.2. Qualitative results
4.2.2.1. A heterogeneity of use in practices
The integration of DMDs for professional use is very heterogeneous:
‒ six CNPs and one expert indicated that they do not use digital tools falling within the framework of the referral, with their scope and needs focusing on remote medical monitoring;
‒ four CNPs and one expert indicated that they use tools of this type occasionally in the context of research or trials;
‒ two CNPs are aware of the use of digital tools without knowing the volume of use within their profession;

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 33
‒ finally, five CNPs and one expert indicated that they use digital tools as part of their routine practice.
It should be noted that within the same CNP, use may also be heterogeneous and target certain subspecialities.
Generally speaking, CNPs in predominantly clinical specialities see themselves as being little concerned by the use of digital medical devices for strictly professional use. For these specialties, digital technology usages are mainly focused on telehealth, in particular remote medical monitoring and applications intended for patients. This trend can also be explained by their hands-on practice and, in particular, the need to combine elements documenting the patient's clinical situation with consideration of the patient's perception of symptoms and the effectiveness of treatments.
Conversely, specialities that are particularly involved in the diagnostic stage, and therefore more measurable, are particularly aware of the evolutions and developments in these technologies. When used, these tools serve as an aid to the interpretation or organisation of the activity, particularly in terms of priority-setting, and remain under human supervision.
Some CNPs report that they are increasingly contacted by manufacturers encouraging them to use DMDs for professional use.
4.2.2.2. Mapping of intended uses
Four intended uses of digital DMs for strictly professional use were identified during these interviews: diagnostic support, screening support, medical decision support and validation support.
While the DMDs mentioned during the exchanges and used as a diagnostic support are all based on artificial intelligence technologies, the DMDs used as a medical decision support reveal a greater variety in the technologies employed, such as augmented reality or smartphone apps. Almost all the specialities indicated that they use applications or tools as diagnostic or medical decision supports (scores, scales, etc.) based on clinically validated decision trees resulting from guidelines published by learned societies or competent authorities. The performances of these applications, which do not use AI and consist of a digital transposition of existing and validated tools, depend on the quality of the information source, the technical development of the tool and the rapid identification of the sources on which recommendations are based.
In terms of how the technology is accessed, two main types of usage emerge:
‒ direct access to the tool with integration within the facility’s own IT system;
‒ more rarely, access to a centralised platform, particularly in radiology, with an algorithm bank available in certain cases. In these cases, professionals do not seem to have systematic information about the metrological performance of the algorithms proposed to them or used by the service provider. This type of access to technology is not the most widespread to date: it was mentioned by two of the CNPs consulted.
4.2.2.3. Barriers to the integration of digital medical devices for professional use
Numerous barriers have been identified, explaining the heterogeneity of practices.
‒ Six CNPs and experts mentioned a growing concern with respect to procedures associated with the use of these tools and their remuneration. In fact, the use of these DMDs may modify or create new practices that are not yet associated with a medical procedure and tariff.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 34
‒ Five CNPs complained that the tools are not always adapted to their practice and therefore not adapted to the needs of professionals for several reasons. Firstly, software is often very hospital-centric and is not adapted to patient follow-up over time, which may explain the fact that the use of DMDs for professional use has not developed in certain specialities in non-hospital practice. Secondly, healthcare professionals are not always involved in the design of software.
‒ The real benefit for the healthcare professional and also for patients is a major concern expressed by these CNPs. Indeed, the desire to maximise certain theoretical performances, to the detriment of practical use, seems to have a negative impact on the use of DMDs. For example, an increased sensitivity of a DMD may result in a high false positive rate and thus lead to an excessive number of unnecessary alerts for healthcare professionals.
Similarly, the patient and user representative association pointed to the “hyperproduction” of data that is not always useful and relevant to care, with quantity sometimes taking precedence over quality.
‒ Finally, the time-consuming nature of digital technology was also reported by some CNPs, who see digital technology as a waste of time to the detriment of time spent with patients. Five CNPs identified interoperability as a major challenge for the integration of professional DMDs, particularly in hospitals, due to the multiplicity of software packages, most of which are speciality- or disease-specific and, so far, not systematically interoperable. Hospital information systems find themselves unable to manage the multitude of software and, in parallel, the necessary security for health data.
‒ Three CNPs cited the high cost and absence of an economic model tailored to these solutions.
‒ Four CNPs noted that the medical reliability of these tools can raise questions and sometimes lead to mistrust of these technologies. This is due to the scarcity or absence of clinical evidence, but also to a lack of consideration of the real benefit to the patient of these new practices.
‒ The young healthcare professionals group highlighted the “black box” effect of tools with embedded artificial intelligence. Indeed, professionals do not have information on the populations of the training sample (which may not be representative of their patient base) and lament the lack of transparency of publishers on the precision of the algorithms or data storage. Another point raised was the lack of transparency about the risk of its use and how the tool has been validated. This raises questions about the possibility of extrapolating the use of these tools from one hospital to another, which is contrary to the desire to standardise practices.
This notion was also raised by the patient and user representative association. The lack of explainability of the results and of transparency on the use of data collected by a DMD can lead to a breakdown in the use of these tools due to patient refusal. Lack of information leads to mistrust, which can adversely affect the dialogue between patients and healthcare professionals.
Other questions were also raised by the CNPs:
‒ issues to be clarified about the medical accountability associated with the use of these DMDs;
‒ an ethical question relating to “unexpected” new information that may be generated by the use of DMDs for professional use and how healthcare professionals can deal with it. For example, a laboratory anomaly detected by an AI algorithm that was not previously identifiable;
‒ the emergence of companies that can directly approach patients for the interpretation of their results;

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 35
‒ six CNPs stressed the role of healthcare professionals in the face of the profound changes brought about by these digital tools and the future of their profession, even if digital technology can be seen as a lever for better organisation of the community-hospital care pathway with a potential delegation of tasks to paramedics;
‒ in the same vein, the patient and user representative association emphasised the importance of strengthening the dialogue between patients and healthcare professionals despite the profound changes brought about by this type of tool. It is important for healthcare professionals to be transparent about their interpretation of the results they provide. For example, if a healthcare professional makes a diagnosis that differs from that of the diagnostic software used, the healthcare professional should be able to explain their interpretation to the patient. The use of these tools must therefore remain under human supervision but the relationship between patients and healthcare professionals must be protected;
‒ Finally, the patient and user representative association noted the need to collect patients' consent in order to improve information and transparency in the use of patients' data.
Ultimately, these obstacles reveal the major challenge of organising the integration of DMDs for professional use and the many questions facing healthcare professionals: how to integrate DMDs into professional practice, what position to give them and how to reorganise care?
4.2.2.4. Choice and selection of digital medical devices for professional use
Reflecting the heterogeneity of practices, the choice and selection of DMDs by professionals is also highly variable.
Since most DMDs are developed for a single speciality, the players driving purchasing choices are the hospital departments directly concerned. Hence, the two expert pharmacists interviewed indicated that they were not consulted or did not have any specific role to assist in choices with respect to these devices, which are primarily made by the departments without their intervention.
This selection of DMDs is based on choice criteria that appear to be poorly formalised in the various organisations reported and not really based on clinical demonstration.
The most cited criteria are as follows:
• ergonomics of the solution;
• performance of the tool indicated by the manufacturer, sometimes supplemented by practical tests on site;
• recommendations between users;
• price and number of licences.
The CNPs interviewed stressed the difficulty of using evidence-based medicine to guide their choice due to the scarcity of available data, especially in the context of real-world use. For example, the small number of robust clinical studies, the rapid development of technologies versus the slowness of their evaluation or the difficulty of extrapolating data from one hospital to another were often mentioned by CNPs.
In addition, many CNPs highlighted the regular updating of software or DMDs, which raises the question of the long-term validity of an evaluation at a given moment of time.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 36
To overcome the lack of available clinical data, CNPs have organised themselves or are in the process of getting organised to evaluate DMDs for professional use. Some CNPs are already autonomous in the assessment of DMDs for professional use. Others are setting up observational studies or monitoring the performance of their tools in real-world settings to ensure that the software is correctly configured. Finally, some are considering the evaluation of these solutions on the basis of clinical, technical, integration, price and maintenance criteria.
4.2.2.5. The role expected of the HAS
The CNPs were specifically asked about their expectations of the HAS on the subject of DMDs for professional use:
‒ thirteen CNPs, five experts, the young professionals group and the patient and user representative association would be in favour of the HAS adopting a position on these subjects;
‒ three CNPs do not want the HAS to get involved in the choice of digital tools;
‒ two CNPs, one expert and two institutional structures did not give an opinion or were not asked about this subject.
Figure 5. CNP responses to the question: “Would you like the HAS to take up the subject of DMDs for professional use?”
Among the CNPs in favour of the HAS playing a supporting role, four different main types of action were highlighted by the CNPs:
‒ the introduction of decision support guides or general standards for these tools;
‒ assistance with technology monitoring and also with respect to expression of the needs of healthcare professionals, based on their practice in the field, to facilitate the upstream identification of relevant technologies;
‒ the implementation of methodological assistance from the HAS within the framework of a partnership with national professional councils, in particular with the production of methodological guides and/or personalised support for the implementation of real-world studies to assess DMDs;
‒ the implementation of “labels” or any other recognition mechanism.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 37
4.3. Discussion
The integration of DMDs for professional use is still in the early stages of its development and remains very variable between different specialities and within the same medical speciality depending on the professionals. In addition, some CNPs found it particularly difficult to visualise the examples of digital medical devices mentioned in the referral. This reflects the heterogeneity of the development of these technologies and practices. There is still little known about the rate of use of these tools on a national scale. Use is very much linked to the financial and material resources of the hospitals or medical facilities, which are variable within France.
The CNPs are facing changes in their profession and are very concerned by digital issues. The interviews made it possible to identify the priority topics for the CNPs. Few CNPs question the performance of DMDs for professional use. However, some have organised their own assessment procedure. For many CNPs, the core of the issue relates to the deployment of telehealth (outside the scope of this project), the lack of an economic model for integrating software into practices, medical accountability and the need for a review of tariffs for procedures involving the use of this type of technology.
Since the use of DMDs for professional use is in its infancy, numerous CNPs stressed the importance of not obstructing access to innovation and allowing usages and associated practices to develop. The variation in the responses with respect to the role expected of the HAS paves the way for several scenarios that can be discussed and worked on.
In summary
The use of DMDs for professional use is very heterogeneous between CNPs. Within the scope of the DMDs covered by this project (excluding telehealth and prescribing support/dispensing support software, in particular), the main usages identified are oriented towards systems designed to aid diagnosis or screening predominantly used by diagnostic specialisations. The integration of these tools also depends on their acceptance by patients. To this end, information on the use of data and greater dialogue between patients and healthcare professionals are essential to ensure confidence in these tools.
The selection of these tools by professionals is not regulated and depends on the profession's appetite for digital technology and the integration of these tools into practices.
71% of the CNPs questioned indicated that they were in favour of support from the HAS, with very variable expectations, described according to decreasing level of involvement:
− the implementation of “labels” or equivalents;
− the implementation of methodological assistance from the HAS within the framework of a partnership with national professional councils, in particular with the production of methodological guides and/or personalised support for the implementation of real-world studies to assess DMDs;
− the implementation of guidelines or standards for these tools;
− assistance with technology monitoring or pre-selection support to facilitate the upstream identification of these tools in order to then propose them to healthcare professionals, based on their practice in the field.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 38
5. Outlook
The analysis of the literature and bilateral meetings with professionals highlights the wide disparity in their expectations of the HAS depending on the specialities, which appear to have differing requirements in terms of decision support for choosing DMDs. On the basis of this observation, the HAS proposes various scenarios concerning its possible role with respect to the promotion of the proper use of these DMDs. The construction of this project with stakeholders, possibly including a call for contributions, will enable other possible outputs to emerge and/or help structure these initial avenues to be explored. The various scenarios identified below could be combined. They are not necessarily mutually exclusive and will be explored in the context of the work that is being launched.
5.1. Output for information purposes for professionals: decision support guide for the selection of digital medical devices for professional use
This generalist guide will aim to guide users and buyers of DMDs for professional use in their choice by enabling them to identify the main key questions to ask about DMDs:
‒ its development: provenance and reliability of the information available, source populations from which the data used are derived;
‒ validation of the tool and the associated performances;
‒ integration in care pathways;
‒ the economic model;
‒ questions concerning maintenance, the fate of data, etc.
These key questions could be accompanied by a list of prior requirements relating to interoperability with information systems, CE marking, ergonomics of the DMD, data storage, etc.
The production of a guide to assist in the selection of these devices is in line with the current position of the NHS on the subject of DMDs for professional use.
Advantages
This decision support guide would be generalist in nature and common to all specialities. It could therefore cover the broad field of DMDs for professional use and be implemented rapidly.
It would be a direct decision support for healthcare professionals or facilities, structuring the key information required to inform their choices.
Disadvantage
This guide would necessarily be of a general nature and would not constitute an assessment.
5.2. Core outputs enabling structuring of an assessment
Two scenarios have been envisaged, at this stage, for the structuring of an evaluation process, but new scenarios may emerge as the work proceeds.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 39
5.2.1. Scenario 1: implementation of a collaborative assessment method, supported by national professional councils
The implementation of a collaborative method could take several possible forms:
1. the production by the HAS of a generalist guide on appropriate assessment methods for DMDs for professional use;
2. the implementation of collaborative technology monitoring by the HAS and the CNP;
3. specific methodological support for CNPs wishing to carry out evaluations of the performance claims of technologies;
4. specific methodological support for CNPs wishing to carry out evaluations of the contribution of the technology compared to practice without these technologies;
5. specific methodological support for CNPs wishing to validate the national deployment of these technologies.
The assessments implemented by the CNPs could consist of:
‒ a (pre-)selection of algorithms with artificial intelligence based on an independent dataset, labelled by the CNPs;
‒ an evaluation based on the implementation of pilot studies for real-world testing of tools over a period of several months for each new software version;
‒ an evaluation based on the mobilisation of the entire profession on a national scale for assessment on a voluntary basis.
The possibility of creating partnerships between several CNPs was raised in order to pool experiences and carry out a collaborative assessment of these solutions with the support of the HAS.
Advantage
This personalised approach would make it possible to support the needs of CNPs in an individualised manner, involving the entire ecosystem.
Disadvantage
The question of feasibility and the resources to be mobilised for the HAS remains to be explored.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 40
5.2.2. Scenario 2: the implementation of a labelling system (or equivalent) or assessment standards by the HAS for DMDs for professional use
Although no requests for a traditional assessment of DMDs for professional use have been formulated by CNPs, some CNPs or experts have requested the development of assessment standards or the implementation of a voluntary labelling system or equivalent based on clinical and organisational criteria. However, the modalities remain to be defined. If this approach were to be implemented, it should be tested on the categories of DMDs that raise the most questions in terms of validation and could be based on a risk matrix constructed in collaboration with professionals.
Advantage
This scenario could address the specific need of certain CNPs and provide a turnkey solution for professionals seeking assistance.
Disadvantages
Given the divergent views between CNPs (and within several CNPs), a mixed reception by the ecosystem is possible.
This assessment must not be a barrier to the development of innovations.
Obtaining a label will have a cost for companies, with an impact on the prices of the DMDs that would be labelled, in a context where the economic model for DMDs has yet to be defined.
The question of feasibility and the resources to be mobilised by the HAS still needs to be explored, given the number of technologies, as well as their turnover and the skills required.
In summary
Two main output types appear to emerge:
‒ the provision of documents aimed at healthcare professionals or facilities, such as generalist decision support guides for the selection of DMDs for professional use;
‒ the development of outputs contributing to the construction of a core framework for the assessment of DMDs for professional use and, in particular:
• the implementation of collaborative processes with national professional councils, to provide varying degrees of methodological support for the assessment of DMDs;
• the implementation of a voluntary labelling mechanism (or equivalent) for DMDs for professional use, by the HAS directly for CNPs seeking this.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 41
6. Conclusion
The inventory carried out within the framework of this work highlights the need for the HAS and the public authorities to adopt a position to promote the proper use of DMDs for professional use. It is necessary to build a framework of trust dedicated to DMDs for professional use, in order to promote the incorporation of those that are useful and innovative into the healthcare system, without acting as a brake on innovation.
As explained in
the scoping document
 for the project, several avenues for exploration emerged during this preliminary work, the practical arrangements of which will need to be defined.
In the short term and in order to support healthcare professionals and facilities in the selection of DMDs, the HAS wishes to publish a decision support guide for the selection of the DMDs for professional use outlined above. This will guide users and buyers by helping them identify the key questions to consider or ask concerning the DMD (development, validation of the tool and associated performance, integration in pathways, economic model, maintenance questions, fate of data, etc.). Other outputs, such as the creation of a risk matrix specific to DMDs for professional use, could also be discussed depending on the needs identified in future discussions with stakeholders. However, the implementation of this type of output remains complex given the very heterogeneous nature of the notion of risk depending on medical specialties and would require the involvement of all stakeholders.
Depending on the needs identified in terms of evolution of the assessment of this type of technology, the HAS may issue new proposals to the public authorities. These reflection processes relating to assessment could, for example, be encouraged in the context of national plans or trials.

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 42
Table of annexes
Annexe 1.
Summary of the responses of INAHTA assessment agencies to the shared questionnaire
43

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 43
Annexe 1. Summary of the responses of INAHTA assessment agencies to the shared questionnaire
Country
Position regarding DMDs for professional use
Germany (G-BA and IQWiG)
Germany assesses DMDs for professional use if they are associated with a diagnostic or therapeutic procedure. These primarily concern DMDs IIb or III. This assessment is mandatory in the non-hospital setting for reimbursement but optional in the context of hospital care. The IQWiG will be responsible for assessment, based on comparative clinical assessment criteria (comparative effectiveness (i.e. additional patient-relevant benefit)). The report produced by the IQWIG will then be used by the G-BA, in addition to ethical, social and legal criteria, in order to reach a decision with respect to public funding of the device and to negotiate its price with the manufacturer.
Austria (AIHTA and GoeG)
The AIHTA is in the process of setting up an optional assessment process for DMDs for professional use, the aim being to provide visibility with respect to the tools used and to categorise them (not in a reimbursement context). The assessment criteria will be clinical, derived from RCTs. The AIHTA envisages a standard assessment method based on PICO analysis. At the time of answering the questionnaire, no assessment had been made on the subject and DMDs for professional use were selected in hospitals directly by the user services.
Canada (CADTH)
The current scope of assessments carried out by the CADTH concerns digital devices that are used for medical purposes liable to have a direct impact on the prevention, diagnosis or treatment of a disease or a condition, including software (for example, artificial intelligence algorithms, mobile apps).
The assessment methods used are conventional HTA methods. However, work is under way to potentially update assessment methods and processes in order to adapt methodologies to DMDs. Assessment is optional (not necessary for integration or reimbursement of devices) and is aimed at informing public decision-makers.
Spain (AQuAS)
The AQuAS states that, in theory, it wishes to assess all DMDs falling with the following scope: mHealth (health applications), robotics, clinical decision support systems, telemedicine services, algorithms. However, in practice, the AQuAS indicates that it does not conduct a full assessment for all these DMDs. This assessment is based on safety, efficacy/efficiency, organisational impact, ethical, legal and social criteria. A specific framework dedicated to DMDs for professional use is under consideration, particularly relative to the areas to be taken into account for the assessment and the level of requirements expected for the studies. On its website, the AQuAS indicates that it is adapting the NICE framework dedicated to DMDs and that it has set up a working group with NICE.
Finland (FinCCHTA)
At present, the FinCCHTA assesses digital MDs (such as mobile applications or web applications) intended for the collection of symptoms by patients themselves or measurement of Patient Reported Outcomes (PRO) in the context of follow-up for the purposes of exchanges with healthcare professionals. The agency states that it could evaluate any type of digital

HAS • Integration of digital medical devices for professional use into clinical practice • November 2022 44
service, whether it is an application, a robot or artificial intelligence. The assessment method dedicated to DMDs is Digi-HTA. The objective of this evaluation is to issue a recommendation for use (with a score from 0 to 10) of a digital solution, which is made public. A score ≤ 4 corresponds to a recommendation to give careful consideration before using the product (an organisation considering the deployment of a product should consider that there are shortcomings in one or more of the key areas) whereas a score of 10 corresponds to a recommendation to use the product.
The recommendations are based on the information provided by companies on their product, supplemented by a literature review, expert assessments and any additional questions for companies. This assessment is optional and is based on criteria related to efficacy, cost, security, confidentiality and data protection, ease of use and usability/accessibility. Although Digi-HTA can be applied to tools with AI, no DMDs for professional use have so far been the subject of an assessment in this context.
Netherlands (ZonMW and ZIN)
DMDs are not assessed in the Netherlands, except in specific cases (in particular, if specially requested by the Ministry or a learned society).
The ZIN, which is responsible for the assessment of health products in the Netherlands, is currently developing a methodological guide aimed at healthcare professionals and buyers to assist them in the generation of evidence and in the clinical evaluation of the added value of DMDs (particularly those with AI).
The ZonMW, which is responsible for health research in the Netherlands, is developing a methodological guide for clinical and medico-economic studies aimed at the health technology industry, and therefore not specific to DMDs for professional use.
Poland (AOTMiT)
The Agency of Health Technology Assessment and Tariff System (AOTMiT) conducts assessments at the request of the Ministry of Health. These may concern all types of DMDs. Their assessment is mandatory for reimbursement and is based on efficacy, safety, cost and budgetary impact criteria. A certificate concerning data security is also required (without assessment by the AOTMiT). For the solutions already evaluated, the AOTMiT states that due to lack of sufficient data or lack of robustness, most of the evaluated DMDs received a negative opinion or additional pivotal studies were requested to collect the necessary data. Among these DMDs, only one DMD for professional use (aid for the early diagnosis of infarction based on ECGs) has been assessed, and received a negative recommendation from the agency.

## p_3448941 : 2023-06-29_dispositif_medicaux_numerique_a_usage_professionnel_guide_daide_au_choix

Guide d’aide au choix des dispositifs médicaux numériques à usage professionnel
À destination des professionnels et des établissements de santé
