HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 4
Background
Numerous digital solutions are used in routine care today. However, their utility or relevance in relation
to the existing arsenal are not always established or known. In particular, this is the case for certain
digital solutions for professional use, whic
h do not fall within the existing evaluation frameworks for
public funding cover. Consequently,
healthcare professionals may use digital medical devices
(DMDs) in the context of a medical procedure without being fully informed about their perfor-
mance or li
mitations or, conversely, be reluctant to use them for these same reasons
.
On the basis of this observation, the HAS proposed t
he
implement
ation of
an approach based on suc-
cessive stages described in a
scoping document published on 31 August 2022
. The scoping document
defines the digital solutions concerned by this project. These are digital technologies:
‒
with the status of
medical device and hence a declared medical purpose
;
‒
used by healthcare professionals
;
‒
and that are not
currently eligible for a national assessment process
.
The scope will therefore focus on
DMDs designed to aid screening, diagnosis, medical decision
-
making or therapeutic decision
-
making,
with DMDs potentially combining several functionalities.
For
example, among the numerous DMDs that exist, the following can be mentioned: diag-
nostic or screening support systems for
pulmonary nodules or tuberculosis based on chest
X
-
rays, cancerous nodules based on mammograms, melanomas based on skin lesion im-
ages, bone fractures based on X
-
rays, diabetic retinopathy based on eye fundus exa
ms or
polyps during colonoscopy, e
tc. As for medical decision support systems, these can be used
to calculate the parameters or methods for implementing a treatment directly based on the
patient's data. Other usages for the prediction of therapeutic response are also developing.
DMDs for remote monitoring, which may be reimbursed in
accordance with common law, and prescrib-
ing or dispensing aid software that have already been the subject of studies at the HAS, do not fall
within the scope of this work.
In this context, in November 2022, the HAS published an inventory in the form of a summary document
entitled “
Integration of digital medical devices for professional use in
to
clinical practice: State of the art
and decision support opportunities
”
. This document
made it possible to draw up an inventory of the
development of DMDs for professional use, their integration into clinical practice by healthcare profes-
sionals and the position of regulators and health technology assessment agencies with respect to these
to
ols. It also highlights the expectations of professionals in terms of decision support and the opportu-
nities that could be worked upon in the context of this project.
The production of a generalist decision support guide for the selection of digital medical devices for
professional use was immediately scheduled as being one of the first outputs to be produced.
The
objectives of this generalist guide are as follows:
1.
to guide healthcare professionals (working in private practice or in healthcare facilities)
and buyers of DMDs for professional use in their choices by enabling them to identify
the main key questions to consider
;
2.
to develop confidence
in the use of DMDs for professional use and to therefore encourage
their integration into the health system where useful and effective.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 5
This generalist guide is not intended to provide specific responses to assess a DMD in partic-
ular or to assign an assessment score to each question
but aims to provide healthcare profession-
als and buyers with elements for consideration in terms of the questions to be asked when making their
choices.
Other outputs will follow, depending on the needs identified in terms of evolution of the assessment of
this type of technology. These reflection processes relating to assessment could, for example, be
tested in the context of trials or national plans.
How should this guide be used?
‒
This guide is
aimed at healthcare professionals (working in private practice or in
healthcare facilities) and hospital purchasing networks or groups
.
‒
The questions in this guide are organised into three main sections: the need, the technology
envisaged and any actions to be put in place prior to the purchase. The questions proposed
are given by way of indication and remain elements to guide the DMD purc
hasing reflection
process.
‒
For each of these questions, the guide specifies the sources available to obtain information to
help answer them. In particular, this guide will enable you to ask the manufacturer or software
developer of the proposed DMD for the information you need to ma
ke an informed choice.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 6
Foreword
1.
What is your medical and technological
need?
‒
What is your need and what problems is the use of this DMD
required to solve? Is this need
shared by all the stakeholders?
‒
What are the intended uses of the DMD and the outcomes that it will provide? For example, will
it help:
•
diagnose a disease?
•
predict or enable early detection of a clinical event, so that patients can be referred to a
specific care pathway?
•
determine a severity score or dose calculation so that a treatment can be adapted?
•
etc.
‒
What are the characteristics of the patient populations and the use contexts concerned by your
need?
2.
Have you identified various digital
medical devices that might meet this
need?
Where can I find this information?
The scientific literature, the specialised press, word of mouth,
feedback from experience,
and
communications by professional organisations are all
methods of
identifying DMDs in your field of specialisation.
Mapping
the different DMDs liable to address your need will enable you to obtain an
overview of the
offering available
.
Background
Your
medical and technological need is the starting point for the proposed question pro-
cess
. Precise identification of the intended uses of the DMD and the patient populations or use
scenarios concerned will make it possible to check that the DMD(s) proposed do indeed constitute
a potential response to your need.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 7
Decision support guide
3.
Does the purpose of the digital medical
device correspond to your intended use?
Background
For each of these digital medical devices, the intended use of the DMD must be compared with your
medical and technological need in order to check whether the intended use complies with the CE
mark
ing
purpose
.
Where can I find this information?
The manufacturer must provide you with the information
required, in particular via the instructions for use of the medical device.
‒
For what use(s) (also called indication(s) or intended purpose(s)
)
1
has the DMD obtained
CE
marking?
‒
What are the characteristics of the target population(s) or what is the target population of the
DMD?
‒
Does the intended purpose of the DMD scheduled by the manufacturer correspond to your in-
tended use?
‒
What are the conditions and/or technical aspects for use of the DMD (for example, prerequisites,
precautions for use, necessary measures, etc.)? Do these correspond to your intended use?
Will you be able to apply the conditions of use indicated in the inst
ructions for use of the DMD?
‒
Are there situations in which the digital medical device must not be used (for example, contra-
indications, warnings, restrictions for use, etc.)?
1
According to (EU) regulation 2017/745 on medical devices, the intended purpose of a medical device corresponds to “
the use for
which a device is intended according to the data supplied by the manufacturer on the label, in the instructions for use or in
promotional or sales materials or statements and as specified by the manufacturer in the clinical evaluation
”.
If you need to make a choice between several available DMDs, we invite you to compare the different
responses to all the questions below (3.1 to 8.15) in order to perform a
multicriteria analysis
and
identify:
‒
the differences between the available digital medical devices;
‒
the most appropriate DMD for your need and your clinical context.
A summary table for comparing the different digital medical devices for professional use is
available in annex 2
.
N.B.
These questions do not constitute marketing requirements. They are intended to help
healthcare professionals and facilities make informed choices: therefore, it is not always necessary
to answer every question.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 8
4.
What evidence do you have of the benefit
of the digital medical device?
Background
Evidence to support manufacturers’ claims is essential. This will enable you to document the clinical
or organisational benefits you expect, depending on the case.
Where can I find this information?
In addition to the published data (scientific publications, etc.)
that can be supplied by the manufacturer in support of its DMD performance and safety claims
,
2
a
targeted search of clinical trial databases provides essential information about ongoing and future
studies, or studies for which the results may not have been published (e.g. clinicaltrials.gov). Do not
hesitate to also contact other professionals or y
our professional organisations to request their
feedback.
4.1.
What is the expected impact of using the digital medical device?
For example, does it concern:
‒
a clinical impact;
‒
an organisational impact;
‒
a budgetary impact;
‒
or another type of impact?
4.2.
What
evidence is available to support the expected impact?
‒
What types of studies have been conducted or are scheduled? All available sources can be
used (e.g. publications, protocols and study report, etc.).
‒
Are the available study results specific to the DMD in question? You can verify the DMDs in-
volved
in the publications/available evidence in order to identify evidence specific to the DMD and
what relates to the state of the art (for example, for a category of technologies).
‒
Are the available studies relevant to document the expected impact?
You should take into con-
sideration the methodological quality of the study design and/or data collection, the research ques-
tion and the results provided
.
3
‒
Do the available data demonstrate an effect?
‒
Is the observed effect relevant or significant?
4.3.
Have other structures already used the digital medical device in
question?
‒
If so, is it possible to contact them?
2
These data may have been used to obtain medical device status (“clinical investigations”).
3
The level of evidence of a study “
characterises the capacity of the study to answer the question posed. The capacity of a study to
answer the question posed is judged on the basis of the correspondence between the study and the work context (question,
population, assessment criteria) and t
he following characteristics: the appropriateness of the study protocol to the question posed;
the existence or otherwise of significant biases in its implementation; the suitability of the statistical analysis to the ob
jectives of the
study; the statistic
al power of the study and, in particular, the sample size
”.
HAS, Niveau de preuve et gradation des recommandations de bonne pratique [Level of evidence and grading of clinical practice guidelines], 2013.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 9
‒
What is the feedback concerning deployment and use of the DMD (performance, modification
of workflows, satisfaction with use, costs, etc.)?
Due to the innovative nature of DMDs for professional use (therefore potentially still little integrated into
routine clinical practice), this type of feedback can be difficult to collect. Nonetheless, the day
-
to
-
day
experience of users, after a period of familiarisation, provides some perspective on the impact of a
DMD for professional use on practices, professions and organisations.
You should take into consideration any potential conflicts of interest of the first users identified (e.g.
participation in the design of the DMD or involvement in the scientific committee, etc.).
4.4.
Have other players assessed the tool (learned societies, assessment
agencies, third
-
party trial sites, etc.)?
Assessments carried out by other players can provide an outside perspective on the DMD. In the same
way as for the previous question, you are invited to take into consideration any potential conflicts of
interest.
4.5.
Is the operation of the digital medical device subject to human
supervision or control?
Human “supervision” or control can take different forms in your organisation (e.g. a comparison
between the recommendations resulting from the algorithm and professional guidelines).
‒
Is human supervision carried out internally by the
manufacturer or potentially by yourself or a
trusted third party? In the case of a trusted third party, is this party clearly identified and
qualified?
‒
Is this supervision finished or is it a continuous process throughout the DMD life cycle?
‒
Has this supervision led to precautions, warnings or vigilance measures being issued in certain
situations? Are the results accessible and transparent for users?
5.
What are the operating principles of the
digital medical device?
Background
This section groups together questions on the operation and technology of the envisaged DMD.
There are two types of questions, depending on the technology on which the DMD is based: general
questions relating to any type of DMD and questions specific to DM
Ds based on machine learning.
Where can I find this information?
The manufacturer must be able to transparently and explicitly
share this information with you.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 10
5.1.
What type of data, and in which format, need to be entered or
supplied for the digital medical device to operate?
‒
What
data are required for the MD to work correctly (e.g. laboratory data, clinical data, imaging
data, etc.)? Are the data purely medical?
‒
Have you checked their availability in your facility’s information system or your professional
software?
‒
What are the methods for collecting and recording the data (in other words, are they automati-
cally collected from existing data, or do they need to be specifically entered by you, by the care
team or by patients themselves, etc.)?
5.2.
Is the main functionality of the digital medical device based on
machine learning
4
?
In addition, for DMDs based on machine learning
5.3.
Do you know the type of
machine learning?
In particular, specify whether the machine learning is:
•
initial machine learning, with an algorithm designed by
learning and then frozen after deployment
of the DMD;
•
incremental, with an algorithm in which updating of the structure and/or parameters after
deployment of the DMD is supervised by a human and involves
a priori
and/or
a posteriori
valida-
tion;
•
continuous, with a system learning autonomously after its deployment.
Note: for technologies with machine learning, the HAS has developed a 42
-
item grid to help
you examine these questions in greater depth if you so wish
.
5
5.4.
Do the operation and results
supplied by the digital medical device
comply with the reference scientific and/or medical recommendations
(clinical guidelines, scientific publications)?
‒
Are the sources used to construct the algorithm clearly identifiable and, if applicable, are they
in line with French practices?
‒
Is the DMD up to date with the latest professional guidelines
?
6
‒
Is there a procedure for updating information and recommendations once the digital medical
device has been purchased?
4
This is a p
rocess whereby an algorithm evaluates and improves its performance without the intervention of a programmer,
repeating its execution on datasets until is regularly obtains relevant results.
5
Grille descriptive des fonctionnalités des dispositifs médicaux embarquant un système avec apprentissage automatique (intelligence artificielle) [Descriptive grid of functionalities of medical devices with an embedded machine learning system (artificial intelligence)], HAS, 2020
6
The aim here is not to know whether the DMD is mentioned in the reference scientific and/or medical recommendations but to ma
ke
sure that the development of the DMD is supported by evidence
-
based medicine and that the data are up to date.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 11
5.5.
Can the recommendations or results proposed by the digital medical
device be interpreted clinically by the healthcare professionals using
it?
‒
Are the recommendations or results produced by the DMD easy to use/analyse and, if so, can
they be explained to patients?
‒
Are there alerts to indicate the DMD's usage limits?
5.6.
Is the operation of the system easy to understand?
‒
Do you know how the decisions or results proposed by the digital medical device are made?
‒
Can the elements taken into account by the DMD in the decision support process be
explained?
5.7.
Could the decisions or results proposed by the digital medical device
be promotional in nature or potentially biased?
This question will not apply to all DMDs but may be essential for some of them. For example, in the
case of DMDs designed to aid therapeutic decision
-
making, the selection, order and presentation of
the proposed treatments must not be influenced by promoti
onal considerations. The information pro-
vided must be neutral, accurate, exhaustive and complete.
5.8.
Do you have access to information concerning the data that were
used for assessment of the digital medical device?
‒
What performance and metric data are available? Performance data include precision, sensitiv-
ity, specificity, AUC (area under the curve), the F1
-
score, etc. These data must be appropriate
to the type of DMD and its intended purpose.
‒
Was an independent and multicentre database used to assess the model? Here, the aim is to
check that the DMD has undergone external validation.
‒
Was the validation dataset sampled in a representative way and did it include extreme cases?
‒
Are the results reproducible from one time to the next using the same data?
5.9.
Has the technology already been validated in a context similar to your
own, i.e. on a patient population, with a data
acquisition method and
environmental conditions similar to those found in clinical practice?
Differences between the data used for learning and the data used in practice can lead to a variation in
the performance of the DMD. Validation of the device’s performance in a context similar to the one in
which you will use it is important.
The aim here is to check how the DMD's theoretical performance may differ from the conditions in
which it is used in your facility:
‒
Different population characteristics in terms of disease prevalence or patient profiles? These
differences may be demographic (age categories, gender, etc.), pathophysiological (pregnancy,
subjects with diabetes or asthma, etc.), morphological, clinical or
biological (disease stage, etc.).
‒
Different data acquisition method (e.g. a device/DMD of another brand or data entered by a
different profile of person, etc.)?

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 12
‒
Different environmental conditions (different patient positioning during medical imaging, etc.)?
5.10.
What data are provided by the manufacturer to attest to the reliability
and stability over time of the digital medical device, once deployed?
The aim is to determine whether the DMD has been tested against input data disturbances and to
identify the procedures put in place by the manufacturer to monitor the quality and safety of the system,
even in extreme or unexpected situations. For example:
‒
What system is in place to detect data anomalies (outliers and data anomalies, sensor malfunc-
tions, stress tests, etc.)?
‒
For algorithms with “continuous” or “on
-
the
-
fly” learning, what measures can be put in place to
identify any deterioration or drift in the algorithm?
5.11.
Does the digital medical device seem to be easy to use and have
satisfactory ergonomics?
‒
Was the DMD co
-
developed with healthcare professionals, as potential users of the device? At
the very least, were healthcare professionals consulted for their expertise during the DMD de-
velopment cycle?
‒
Has the satisfaction of user healthcare professionals been evaluated?
‒
Is the level of adoption of the DMD by users good? Are there scientific publications to illustrate
this?
‒
How is user feedback managed? Are you going to be able to offer feedback to allow changes
to be made to the software?
6.
What would be the organisational and
environmental consequences of
introducing the digital medical device?
Background
This section asks questions about the possible consequences of introducing the envisaged DMD,
such as the direct impact on your professional practice and environment, and the indirect
impacts,
particularly on patient care and the societal impact of the DMD.
Where can I find this information?
In addition to the information provided by the manufacturer,
information specific to your own practical organisation or that of your
facility, where applicable, will
be useful in answering all these questions.
6.1.
What would be the impact or indirect benefit on patient care?
‒
Will the use of the DMD lead to differences in patient care (new constraints, simplification of the
patient pathway, changes in the community medicine
-
hospital relationship, etc.)?

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 13
‒
Are there any data on the satisfaction of patients for whom professionals have used the device,
either positive or negative?
6.2.
What organisational impact
7
would the digital medical device have in
your professional environment?
‒
How will the device fit into the current workflow?
•
Who will use the DMD? Will the DMD user be responsible for implementing the action rec-
ommended by the device?
•
Will the workflow of the users involved in the operation of the device be modified?
•
Will its use involve coordination between healthcare professionals and/or a shift in responsi-
bilities between healthcare professionals?
•
Will the DMD modify some of the tasks currently performed by the team (modification and
transfer of competence between healthcare professionals with regard to the person, time or
information available; creation or deletion of a task)? Could some of these t
asks or alerts be
delegated to the manufacturer?
•
Will additional resources be required to use the tool (specific equipment, additional profes-
sional, etc.)?
•
What is the average time required for use of the device?
‒
What resources would need to be deployed to support teams in learning how to use the device
if it is chosen?
•
Will all future users of the DMD be involved in the choice of DMD (model, type)? Are you
confident that the DMD will gain the support of future users?
•
On average, how long will it take to install the product?
•
Is there a learning time to master its use?
•
Is any training or induction for users planned to help them learn how to use the device? Does
the manufacturer provide ongoing technical training and/or personalised support throughout
the duration of use?
‒
What is the impact on the working conditions and health of healthcare professionals?
•
Does the DMD have an impact on the health of user professionals (for example, a DMD that
would have a positive or negative impact on the stress levels or mental workload of
healthcare professionals, or a DMD that would reduce or eliminate the time spent by
profes-
sionals in contact with a high
-
risk device, etc.)?
•
Can users configure alarms according to their relevance and your needs in your professional
practice?
7
According to the HAS’ organisational impact map for health technology assessment, three types of organisational impact can be
encountered: impacts with a direct effect on the components of the care process, impacts that affect the skills and capabilit
ies
required
by the players involved to implement the care process and impacts on the general population.
Organisational impact map for health
technology assessment, HAS, 2020
.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 14
6.3.
What is the societal and environmental impact of the
digital medical
device?
‒
What impact will introducing the digital medical device have on access to care? The incorpora-
tion of a DMD can have an impact on equality of care or accessibility for individuals by integrat-
ing sociocultural, ethical, socioeconomic, geographical and digita
l divide issues, etc. In your
practice, you may be able to assess the rates of care by gender or age, or the rate of refusal of
care, for example.
‒
What is the environmental impact of the DMD
?
8
In particular, you could assess the costs of any
damage caused to the environment, ecosystems and indirectly to users. For example, this im-
pact could relate to the carbon costs of materials or changes in greenhouse gas emissions.
8
For more information: several online resources are available, such as the websites of the
mission interministérielle numérique
écoresponsable [interministerial mission for eco
-
responsible digital technology],
the French Agency for Ecological Transition
(
ADEME
), or on the
RESAH
(Hospital Buyers Network) website.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 15
7.
What are the costs and economic
consequences of using the digital
medical device?
These questions involve considering how you could finance the DMD, what the costs are of acquiring
it, using it and, if necessary, uninstalling it, and the financial consequences (expenditure and/or sav-
ings) for your facility as a result of using it.
7.1.
What financing options are available for the digital medical device?
‒
Could the DMD potentially be funded by a financial contribution linked to its use (e.g. reimburse-
ment by the French National Health Insurance system, valued associated procedure, etc.)?
‒
Is financing achieved through a reduction in expenditure (e.g. reallocation of professionals' time,
etc.) and/or an increase in revenue (e.g. an increase in the number of procedures or an im-
provement in patient satisfaction and the attractiveness of the fa
cility)?
‒
Other financing methods
9
including institutional.
7.2.
What are the contractualisation methods for the digital medical
device?
‒
Will a purchase model be used (permanent access to the software)? a subscription or rental
model? a pay
-
per
-
use model? another business model?
7.3.
What are the costs associated with the digital medical device for the
duration of the contract and the anticipated volume of use?
Depending on the type of DMD, you may have questions about some or all of the following points.
‒
In view of the information presented in question 7.2, what is the gross cost of
10
the DMD (if
applicable)?
‒
What are the costs of implementing the DMD (e.g. trial phase, data collection and storage,
servers, training, organisational changes, etc.)?
‒
What are the operating costs of the device (e.g. payment to the software developer, additional
personnel, etc.)?
‒
Are maintenance and support included in the purchase cost of the DMD?
‒
Is initial training included in the purchase cost of the DMD? What about ongoing training or
training required during use in the event of changes to the team (e.g. every six months for interns
required to use the tool, etc.)?
‒
What are the costs associated with uninstalling the DMD?
9
Other financing methods are possible: trials, sponsorship, calls for tender, public investment programmes, etc.
10
The gross cost is the purchase cost excluding the cost of training, maintenance, installation, etc. It may be a one
-
off cost (for
example, purchasing of a licence) or a regular cost (for example, rental or subscription).

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 16
7.4.
In addition to the medico
-
economic information available in the
scientific literature (e.g. published cost
-
effectiveness and/or cost
-
utility studies, etc.), has a budget impact analysis
11
or a cost
-
consequence analysis been performed?
‒
If so, by whom (e.g. the manufacturer directly, another player, your facility, etc.)?
‒
Is this analysis on a national or local scale?
‒
Can the budget impact analysis be transposed to your context?
7.5.
What are the expected economic consequences following the
purchase of the device?
‒
Will the use of the DMD result in savings in terms of time saved and/or equipment and/or con-
sumables not used (for example, time saved for a consultation, change in the number of proce-
dures, healthcare consumption avoided, etc.)?
‒
Is it possible to share the use of the DMD with other healthcare professionals or one or more
healthcare facilities?
‒
Do you expect the savings generated to be greater than the spending generated by the DMD?
8.
What IT, legal and regulatory resources
would you need to operate this digital
medical device?
Background
The aim of this section is to help you consider the resources you will need to ensure the DMD
operates correctly, prior to acquisition, focusing on three areas: IT requirements, data security is-
sues, and regulatory and legal issues.
Where can I find this information?
In addition to the information provided by the manufacturer,
information specific to your own facility and the organisation of care will be useful in answering all
these questions.
Technical and IT information
Where can I find this
information?
It is essential to discuss the DMD's compatibility with your
network and IT infrastructure with your Information Systems Department (ISD) or IT service provider
before you buy the device.
11
Budget impact analysis is an approach that estimates the short or medium
-
term financial consequences of purchasing a DMD on
the budget of one (or more) funder(s). This estimation measures the difference in costs (additional spending or savings made)
be-
twe
en care with or without the DMD.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 17
8.1.
Is the digital medical device compatible and interoperable with the
existing IT equipment and infrastructure?
It is important to identify whether the DMD can be integrated into the existing infrastructure and work-
flows, in terms of the data required to operate the device, the documents generated, the data transmit-
ted, etc. Selecting a DMD for professional use that
is not interoperable with your equipment and
infrastructure can have repercussions in terms of costs, time, additional workload for users, and even
security risks. Depending on the DMD, a number of questions may emerge. These could include:
‒
Does the DMD comply with the standards set by the French Agency for Digital Health (ANS)
?
12
These standards include:
Identifiant Numérique de Santé
(INS
-
digital health ID),
Pro Santé
Connect
, the Secure
Health Messaging system (MSS) in the event of exchanges between pro-
fessionals, etc.
‒
Is the DMD compatible with the shared medical record (SMR)?
‒
Do the shared documents comply with the interoperability framework for health information sys-
tems (CI
-
SIS)?
8.2.
Are there any minimum requirements and prerequisites to ensure the
digital medical device operates properly?
The minimum requirements or prerequisites may consist of external resources (e.g. collection acces-
sories or installation of a sensor) or internal resources (e.g. storage capacity, a specific version of
professional software) or a combination of both (e.g.
interoperability with the facility's other information
systems).
‒
What technical specifications are required for
the
correct operation of the digital medical device?
8.3.
Is maintenance of the digital medical device by the manufacturer
scheduled at the time of purchase?
‒
Has the manufacturer included a maintenance strategy in its procedures? If so, can it describe
it (e.g. type of procedure, device lifespan, etc.)?
8.4.
Is a digital medical device update scheduled?
‒
If so, what is the
frequency of
algorithm and recommendation update
s
?
‒
Is this provided for in the cost of the device?
‒
How has management of vulnerabilities been incorporated into the device update
process?
8.5.
How does the digital medical device fit into existing cybersecurity
plans?
‒
What additional challenges and concerns are imposed on the current organisation?
‒
Are external resources required to operate it?
‒
What measures are put in place to prevent cyberattacks (intrusion tests carried out regularly by
audit firms, correction of identified vulnerabilities, etc.) or in the event of a cyberattack on the IT
system or device?
12
To date, no specific security standards for DMDs for professional use have been developed by the ANS. However, all the core
standards set by the ANS may apply to these technologies.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 18
‒
Does the DMD comply with the ANS General health information system security policy (PGSSI
-
S), guaranteeing a minimum level of IT security of the DMD?
8.6.
Can the digital medical device work in failsafe mode?
In the event of an incident affecting the IT system or the DMD, it is important to know what the impact
would be on business continuity.
Information concerning data protection and data security
Where can I find this information?
This information can be obtained in the first instance from your
Information Systems Department (ISD),
D
ata
P
rotection
O
fficer (DPO), Information Systems
Security Manager and other IT service providers.
The manufacturer should be able to provide the technical documentation relating to the DMD and, if
it is involved in the maintenance of the DMD, the technical, legal and organisational measures
implemented to guarantee the protection of confidentiality
,
13
data protection and privacy. You could
also ask the manufacturer if it is able to provide a data protection impact assessment document
.
14
Information concerning data protection
Health data are sensitive data within the meaning of the GDPR and warrant the implementation of
special protection
measures (see paragraphs 8.9 et seq.).
8.7.
How will you inform patients about the use of a digital medical
device?
Patient information is an important component in the framework of trust that needs to be built up for
DMDs for professional use.
In application of the French Public Health Code, healthcare professionals are required to inform patients
about their state of health. In particular, patients must be provided with the following information: “the
various investigations, treatments or preve
ntive actions proposed, their usefulness, any urgency, their
consequences, the frequent or serious risks normally foreseeable that they entail, as well as the other
possible solutions and the foreseeable consequences in the event of refusal”
.
15
In this context, physicians should first ensure that they can easily inform patients, in terms adapted to
their level of understanding, about how the DMD works and its usefulness in comparison with other po-
tential tools.
Once patients have been provided with this information, physicians must obtain their consent, particularly
when use of the DMD will result in a medical procedure. When using a DMD, physicians must ensure
that they can easily ask the patient to consent to i
ts use and to the data collection that usually accompa-
nies such use.
13
Article L. 1110
-
4 of the French Public Health Code.
14
Privacy impact assessment tool based on two pillars:
-
the “non
-
negotiable” fundamental principles and
rights laid down by law. These may not be altered in any way, regardless
of the nature, seriousness or likelihood of the risks incurred;
-
management of risks relative to the privacy of the individuals concerned, which makes it possible to determine the appro-
priate technical and organisational measures to protect personal data.
15
Article L. 1111
-
2 of the French Public Health Code.

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 19
In this respect, the French National Medical Association highlights the fact
16
that:
‒
“treating or performing a procedure on a patient without their consent is an act of misconduct for
which a physician may be held civilly liable and subject to disciplinary action”;
‒
although consent must be explicit, there is no requirement for it to be provided in writing; however,
“consent must be given manifestly and clearly, verbally”;
‒
base on case law of judicial and administrative courts, this consent may
be withdrawn by the
patient at any time, as expressly provided for by the legislator
”.
17
By way of clarification, when a “healthcare professional decides to use a medical device involving the
processing of algorithmic data, in which learning has been
based on big data for a preventive, diagnostic
or care procedure,” they must ensure that “the person has been informed and, where appropriate, ad-
vised about the resulting interpretation”
.
18
8.8.
How will you inform patients about the collection and processing of
their personal data?
The operation and optimisation of DMDs are based on the collection and processing of individual health
data from patients who have given their consent.
Hence, to promote transparency and enable tracking, the use of a DMD for professional use and the
associated processing of personal data (information provided for in Article 13 of the GDPR
)
19
must be
indicated in the consultation medical report given to the individual.
The user must individually inform patients about the scheduled use of their personal data prior to the use
of a DMD, in application of regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council of
27 April 2016 on the protection of
natural persons with regard to t
he processing of
personal data and on
the free
movement of such data (hereinafter referred to as the “GDPR”) and the amended French law
No. 78
-
17 of 6 January 1978 relative to data protection (hereinafter referred to as the “French
D
ata
P
ro-
tection
L
aw”). For example, the user must inform them of the identity of the data controller, what data
concerning them will be collected, the intended use of the data (i.e. what processing will be carried out
on the data), for what purpose(s), whether the data wil
l be stored and, if so, for how long, and the rights
the individual has under the GDPR (access, objection, rectification, erasure, restriction).
As a reminder, if users of a DMD wish to reuse the patient data collected, for example for research
purposes, they must first inform the patients concerned
.
20
In some cases, they must comply with a stand-
ard defined by the French Data Protection Authority (CNIL) or obtain its authorisation.
The questions below are intended to help you take into account the regulations applicable to the
protection of personal data (GDPR and the aforementioned French Data Protection Law) concerning
the use and potential reuse of the data collected.
16
Conseil national de l’ordre des médecins, Recueillir le consentement de mon patient [French National Medical Association, Obtai-ning my patient’s consent]
17
Article L. 1111
-
4 of the French Public Health Code.
18
Article L. 4001
-
3 of the French Public Health Code.

CNIL, Recherche médicale : quel est le cadre légal ?
[Medical research: what is the legal framework?]

HAS • Decision support guide for digital medical devices for professional use • June 2023 20
8.9.
Will the data collected during the use of the digital medical device in
your organisation be used by the manufacturer to improve its
algorithm and/or develop other products?
‒
Will the data that you will collect using the DMD be reused by the manufacturer? If so, for what
purposes?
‒
What procedure will be put in place to inform patients of this reuse in advance
21
? Will you be
asked to
transmit the associated information leaflet?
‒
If applicable, will patients be required to give their consent? If so, is the consent freely
given,
specific, in writing and unequivocal?
As a reminder, authorisation for the reuse of the data by the manufacturer must be given in advance,
for each purpose and in writing. It is possible to refuse the reuse envisaged by the manufacturer if you
consider that such reuse is not necessary for use
of the DMD.
Information concerning data security
The aim of this question is to consider how to minimise the risks to individuals and guarantee a level
of security appropriate to the risks to their privacy. To ensure that the use of the digital medical device
complies with the GDPR, it is important to pr
ovide a sufficient level of data security.
The use of products that comply with the regulations is essential. The service provider (the
manufacturer) must be able to guarantee the compliance of its product and you are entitled to
request information on the security measures implemented.
8.10.
What security measures are scheduled or need to be deployed?
‒
What data are used by the DMD (identifying data, pseudonymised data) and who has access
to these data?
‒
What is the data life cycle? Here, the aim is
to
identify data flows: collection, storage, transfer,
archiving, use, reuse, etc.
‒
Are data backups planned, either locally or by a data host? If so, how frequently? These back-
ups must be encrypted and are necessary, whatever the storage method.
‒
What authentication method is planned for the user (e.g. minimum two
-
factor authentication,
etc.)? Who carries out the authentication (facility, supplier, etc.)?
‒
Are actions tracked (or logged): data access, consultation, modification, deletion, etc.?
‒
If the product is deactivated, uninstalled or reaches the end of its life, what will happen to the
data stored outside your organisation's systems: will they be deleted or archived? Will access
of the supplier's authorised personnel to the part of your org
anisation's infrastructure dedicated
to the operation of the DMD, particularly for maintenance purposes, be revoked? Have reversi-
bility and transferability mechanisms been put in place to enable another software developer to
potentially
use the data for th
e facilit
y
?
‒
Are database exports possible, including for maintenance operations? If so, what security
measures are in place to govern these (prior authorisation, compulsory information of the data
manager)? These measures may be legal, technical or organisational.

Legal and regulatory information
Information related to liability
8.13.
How is liability related to the use of digital medical devices shared
between user healthcare professionals and the manufacturer?
This question may arise, for
example, in cases where the decision or result proposed by the digital
medical device leads to harm (for example, incorrect treatment of the patient concerned, incorrect di-
agnosis, etc.).
At present, the legislation in force is not appropriate to the issues raised by machine learning, and in
most situations the
user
healthcare professional
is liable.
23
On the other hand, European law is
examining the issue and is tending towards greater consideration of the liability of manufacturers
.
24
In general, healthcare professionals have an obligation
to the patient to maintain intellectual independ-
ence with respect to the decision or result proposed by the DMD and to document cases of disagree-
ment
.
25
In order to limit their liability, in their dealings with manufacturers, user healthcare professionals:

In cases where the healthcare professional's liability is sought by the patient, the user and/or their

.

-

Before integration
9.
Is a trial phase
required
to test the
performance of the digital medical device
before buying and
to verify the impact of
introducing it before large
-

-
out
?
Although it is not systematic, for some DMDs, a trial phase (e.g. local or reduced deployment, in a test
environment or “sandbox”, method validation, third
-
party trial sites, etc.) can allow you to verify what
the
specific
impacts of introducing the DMD into your practice would be.
‒

main objective
of this trial phase?
‒
How will you
evaluate the success
of this trial?
•
What are the hypotheses to be validated?
•
What are the conditions for validating them?
•
How will you measure the success of this trial and the clinical/organisational impact of adding
this solution (e.g. introduction of time monitoring indicators, growth in business, human re-
sources, quality of care, cost, etc.)?
‒
What
type of evaluation contract
could be put in place with the manufacturer?
‒
What
financial resources are required
to carry out this trial?
This type of feedback from experiments can be extremely useful to share with your entire professional
ecosystem, or even via a scientific publication if possible, to limit their multiplication and allow as many
people as possible to benefit.
10.
For you, the integration of the digital
medical device would be successful if…
In order to assess whether the use of the digital medical device is a “success” and to ensure that users
support its use in the long term, we recommend that you define monitoring indicators before installing
the DMD, so that an objective assessment can be
made, and to determine the frequency with which
these indicators will be analysed. For example, these factors could include the rate of adoption of the
DMD by users, the number and frequency of uses over time, the impact on operations and on the care
pathw
ay, time savings for healthcare professionals, etc.
In
the event of discontinuation of use or failure to integrate the DMD, this information provides valuable
feedback for manufacturers and other potential users.
